A Coinductive Reformulation of Milner’s Proof System for Regular Expressions Modulo Bisimilarity
Clemens Grabmayer ORCID:0000-0002-2414-1073

Abstract
. Milner defined an operational semantics for regular expressions as finite-state processes. In order to axiomatize bisimilarity of regular expressions under this process semantics, he adapted Salomaa’s complete proof system for equality of regular expressions under the language semantics. Apart from most equational axioms, Milner’s system Mil inherits from Salomaa’s system a non-algebraic rule for solving fixed-point equations. Recognizing distinctive properties of the process semantics that render Salomaa’s proof strategy inapplicable, Milner posed completeness of the system Mil as an open question. As a proof-theoretic approach to this problem we characterize the derivational power that the fixed-point rule adds to the purely equational part Mil - of Mil . We do so by means of a coinductive rule that permits cyclic derivations that consist of a finite process graph (maybe with empty steps) that satisfies the layered loop existence and elimination property LLEE, and two of its Mil - -provable solutions. By adding this rule instead of the fixed-point rule in Mil , we define the coinductive reformulation cMil as an extension of Mil - . For showing that cMil and Mil are theorem equivalent we develop effective proof transformations from Mil to cMil , and vice versa. Since it is located half-way in between bisimulations and proofs in Milner’s system Mil , cMil may become a beachhead for a completeness proof of Mil . This article extends our contribution to the CALCO 2021 proceedings. Here we refine the proof transformations by framing them as eliminations of derivable and admissible rules, and we link coinductive proofs to a coalgebraic formulation of solutions of process graphs.

1.
Introduction

Milner introduced in
[
Mil84
]
a process semantics
\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket_{\hskip-1.5ptP}
for regular expressions
e
as finite-state process graphs
\llbracket{e}\rrbracket_{\hskip-1.5ptP}
.
Informally the process interpretation is defined as follows,
for regular expressions built from the constants
0
and
1
by using the regular operators
+
,
{\cdot}
, and
{(\cdot)^{*}}
:

•

0
stands for deadlock,
1
for successful termination, letters
a
for atomic actions,

•

the operators
+
and
{\cdot}
are interpreted as choice and concatenation of processes, respectively,

•

(unary) Kleene star
{(\cdot)^{*}}
denotes iteration with the option to terminate successfully
before each execution of the iteration body
(then even infinitely many iterations are possible).

Milner called regular expressions ‘star expressions’ when they are interpreted as processes.
He formulated this semantics after developing a complete equational proof system for equality of ‘a class of regular behaviors’. By that he understood
the bisimilarity equivalence classes of finite-state processes that are represented by
\mu
-terms.
Then he defined the process semantics by interpreting regular expressions as
\mu
-term representations of finite-state processes.
In doing so, he defined ‘star behaviors’,
the bisimilarity equivalence classes of the interpretations of star expressions
as a subclass of ‘regular behaviors’.
As an afterthought to the complete proof system for regular behaviors,
he was interested in
an axiomatization of equality of ‘star behavior’ directly on star expressions (instead of on
\mu
-term representations).
For this purpose he appropriately adapted Salomaa’s complete proof system
[
Sal66
]
for language equivalence on regular expressions
to a system
Mil
that is sound for equality of denoted star behaviors.
But Milner noticed that completeness of
Mil
cannot be shown in analogy to Salomaa’s completeness proof.
He formulated completeness of
Mil
as an open problem,
because he realized a significant difficulty
due to a peculiarity by which the process semantics contrasts starkly with the language semantics of regular expressions.

The process semantics of regular expressions is incomplete in the following sense. While for every finite-state automaton
M
there is a regular expression
e

whose language interpretation
\llbracket{e}\rrbracket_{L}
coincides with the language accepted by
M
(formally
{L}(\hskip-0.5pt{M}\hskip-0.5pt)=\llbracket{e}\rrbracket_{L}
),
it is not the case that every finite-state process is the process interpretation of some star expression,
not even modulo bisimilarity.
Giving a counterexample that demonstrates this,
Milner proved in
[
Mil84
]
that the process graph
G_{1}
below with linear recursive equational specification
{\mathcal{S}}(\hskip-0.5pt{G_{1}}\hskip-0.5pt)

does not define a star behavior,
and hence is not bisimilar to the process interpretation of a star expression.
He conjectured that the same is true for the process graph
G_{2}
below
with specification
{\mathcal{S}}(\hskip-0.5pt{G_{2}}\hskip-0.5pt)
.
That was confirmed later by Bosscher
[
Bos97
]
.

Y_{1}
G_{2}

Y_{2}&{}=1+b\,{{\cdot}}\,Y_{1}\end{aligned}\,\right.

X_{2}&{}=a_{1}\,{{\cdot}}\,X_{1}+a_{3}\,{{\cdot}}\,X_{3}\\
X_{3}&{}=a_{1}\,{{\cdot}}\,X_{1}+a_{2}\,{{\cdot}}\,X_{2}\end{aligned}\,\right.

(Here and later we highlight the start vertex of a process graph by a brown arrow
,
and emphasize a vertex
v
with immediate termination in brown as
including a boldface ring.)
It follows that
the systems of
{\mathcal{S}}(\hskip-0.5pt{G_{1}}\hskip-0.5pt)
and
{\mathcal{S}}(\hskip-0.5pt{G_{2}}\hskip-0.5pt)
of guarded equations with star expressions cannot be solved by star expressions modulo bisimilarity.
Due to soundness of
Mil
, also the specifications
{\mathcal{S}}(\hskip-0.5pt{G_{1}}\hskip-0.5pt)
and
{\mathcal{S}}(\hskip-0.5pt{G_{2}}\hskip-0.5pt)
are unsolvable by star expressions
when equality is interpreted as provability in
Mil
.
However, if all actions in the process graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are replaced by a single action
a
, obtaining graphs
G_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}
and
G_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}
,
then the arising specifications
{\mathcal{S}}(\hskip-0.5pt{G_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}}\hskip-0.5pt)
and
{\mathcal{S}}(\hskip-0.5pt{G_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}}\hskip-0.5pt)
are solvable, modulo bisimilarity,
and also with respect to provability in
Mil
.
Indeed it is easy to verify that solutions are obtained
by letting
X_{1}\mathrel{{:=}}X_{2}\mathrel{{:=}}X_{3}\mathrel{{:=}}{{a^{*}}}\mathrel{{\cdot}}{0}
in
{\mathcal{S}}(\hskip-0.5pt{G_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}}\hskip-0.5pt)
,
and by letting
Y_{1}\mathrel{{:=}}Y_{2}\mathrel{{:=}}{a^{*}}
in
{\mathcal{S}}(\hskip-0.5pt{G_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}}\hskip-0.5pt)
.

The extraction procedure of solutions of specifications in Salomaa’s proof completeness is able to solve every linear system of recursion equations,
independently of the actions occurring.
It follows that an analogous procedure is not possible for solving systems of linear recursion equations in the process semantics.
The extraction procedure for linear specifications with respect to the language semantics
is possible because both laws for distributing
{\cdot}
over
+
are available, and indeed are part of Salomaa’s proof system.
But
Mil
does not contain
the
left
-distributivity law
x\cdot(y+z)=x\cdot y+x\cdot z
, because it famously is not sound under bisimilarity.
In the presence of only
right
-distributivity
(x+y)\cdot z=x\cdot z+y\cdot z
in
Mil

the extraction procedure from Salomaa’s proof does not work,
because failure of
left
-distributivity oftentimes prevents expressions to be rewritten
in such a way that the fixed-point rule
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
in
Mil
can be applied successfully.
But if
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
is replaced in
Mil
by a general unique-solvability rule scheme for guarded systems of equations
(see Definition
2
),
then a complete system arises (noted in
[
Gra06
]
).
Therefore completeness of
Mil
hinges on whether the fixed-point rule
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
enables to prove equal
any two star-expression solutions of a given guarded system of equations,
on the basis of the purely equational part
Mil
-
of
Mil
.

As a stepping stone for tackling this difficult question, we characterize
the derivational power that the fixed-point rule
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt

adds to the subsystem
Mil
-
of
Mil
.
We do so by means of ‘coinductive proofs’ whose shapes have
the ‘loop existence and elimination property’
LEE
from
[
GF20a
]
.
This property stems from the interpretation of (
1
-free) star expressions,
which is defined by induction on syntax trees, creating a hierarchy of ‘loop subgraphs’. Crucially for our purpose, linear guarded systems of equations that correspond to finite process graphs with
LEE

are
uniquely solvable modulo provability in
Mil
-
.
The reason is that process graphs with
LEE
,
which need not be in the image of the process semantics,
are amenable to applying
right
-distributivity and the rule
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
for an extraction procedure like in Salomaa’s proof (see Section
6
).
These process graphs can be expressed modulo bisimilarity by some star expression,
which can be used to show that any two solutions modulo
Mil
-
of a specification of
LEE
-shape are
Mil
-provably equal.
This is a crucial step in the completeness proof by Fokkink and myself in
[
GF20a
]

for the tailored restriction
BBP
of Milner’s system
Mil
to ‘
1
-free’ star expressions.

Thus motivated, we define a ‘LLEE-witnessed coinductive proof’ as
a process graph
\mathcal{G}
with ‘layered’
LEE
(
LLEE
) whose vertices are labeled by equations between star expressions.
The left- and the right-hand sides of the equations in the vertices of
\mathcal{G}

have to form a solution vector of a specification corresponding to the process graph
\mathcal{G}
.
That specification, however, needs to be satisfied only up to provability in
Mil
-
from sound assumptions.
Such coinductive derivations are typically circular,
like the one depicted in Figure
1

of the semantically valid equation
(a+b)^{*}\cdot 0\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}(a\cdot(a+b)+b)^{*}\cdot 0\,
.
That example is intended to give a first impression of the concepts involved,
despite of the fact that some details can only be appreciated later,
when this example will be revisited in Example
5
. We describe these concepts below.

The process graph
\mathcal{G}
in Figure
1
,
which is given together with a labeling
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
that is a ‘LLEE-witness’ of
\mathcal{G}
.
The
colored
transitions with marking labels
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,1}[n]}}
, for
n\in{\rm Nature}^{+}
,
indicate the LLEE-structure of
\mathcal{G}
, see Section
4
.
The graph
\mathcal{G}
underlies the coinductive proof on the left (see Example
5
for a justification).

\mathcal{G}
is a ‘
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-chart’ that is,
a process graph with
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-transitions that represent empty steps.
Here and later we depict
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-transitions as dotted arrows.
For
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts, ‘
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation’ is the adequate concept of bisimulation (Definition
2
).

We showed in
[
Gra21c
,
Gra20
]

that the process (chart) interpretation
{\mathcal{C}}(\hskip-0.5pt{e}\hskip-0.5pt)
of a star expression
e
is
the image of a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-chart
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}(\hskip-0.5pt{e}\hskip-0.5pt)
with
LLEE
under a functional
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation.
In this example,
\mathcal{G}={\underline{\mathcal{C}}}(\hskip-0.5pt{h^{*}\cdot 0}\hskip-0.5pt)
maps by a functional
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation to interpretations of both expressions in the conclusion. The correctness conditions for such coinductive proofs are formed by the requirement that the left-, and respectively, the right-hand sides of formal equations
form ‘
Mil
-
-provable solutions’ of the underlying process graph: an expression at a vertex
v

can be reconstructed, provably in
Mil
-
, from the transitions to, and the expressions at, immediate successor vertices of
v
.
Crucially we establish in Section
6
,
by a generalization of arguments in
[
GF20a
,
GF20b
]
using
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
,
that every LLEE-witnessed coinductive proof over
Mil
-
can be transformed into a derivation in
Mil
with the same conclusion.

This raises the question of whether the fixed-point rule
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
of
Mil
adds any derivational power to
Mil
-

that goes beyond those of LLEE-witnessed coinductive proofs over
Mil
-
, and if so, how far precisely.
In Section
7
we show that every instance of the fixed-point rule
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
can be mimicked by a LLEE-witnessed coinductive proof over
Mil
-
in which also the premise of the rule may be used.

It follows that the derivational power that
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
adds to
Mil
-
within
Mil
consists of iterating such LLEE-witnessed coinductive proofs
along finite (meta-)prooftrees.
The example in Figure
2

is intended to give a first idea
of the construction that we will use (in the proof of Lemma
35
)
to mimic instances of
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
.
Here this construction results in a coinductive proof that only differs slightly from the one with the same underlying LLEE -
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-chart we saw earlier.
We will revisit this example in Example
7
.

Based on the two transformations from coinductive proofs to derivations in
Mil
, and of applications of the fixed-point rule to coinductive proofs,
we reformulate Milner’s system
Mil

as a theorem-equivalent proof system
cMil
.
For this, we replace the fixed-point rule
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
in
Mil

with a rule that permits to infer an equation
e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}f
from a finite set
\Gamma
of equations
if there is a LLEE-witnessed coinductive proof over
Mil
-
plus the equations in
\Gamma

that has conclusion
e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}f
.
We also define a theorem-equivalent system
CLC
(‘combining LLEE-witnessed coinductive provability’) with the equational coinductive proof rule alone.
In the formalization of these systems
we depart from the the exposition in
[
Gra21a
,
Gra21b
]
.
There, we used a hybrid concept of formulas that included entire coinductive proofs,
which then could be used as specific rule premises.
Here, the proof systems are purely equational, and coinductive proofs occur only as side-conditions of rules that formalize coinductive provability.

Additionally, we formulate proof systems
\overline{\text{\sf cMil}}
and
CC
that arise from
cMil
and
CLC
by dropping ‘
LLEE
-witnessed’ as a requirement for coinductive proofs.
These systems are (obviously) complete for bisimilarity of process interpretations,
because they can mimic the unique solvability rule scheme for guarded systems of specifications mentioned before.

Inspiration for cyclic proofs from related and previous work.

Apart from their origin from a question in process theory,
the results described here were inspired by coinductively motivated proof systems with derivations of cyclic form,
and by our previous work on their proof-theoretic links to traditional equational proof systems.
This is a brief account of those direct influences, without drawing wider connections to work on cyclic proofs.

About proofs by cyclic arguments that express bisimulations we learned
from Rutten and Jacobs
[
JR97
,
Rut98
]
,
and about formalized cyclic derivations
via the coinductively motivated proof systems by Brandt and Henglein
[
BH98
]
:

\textsf{BH}^{=}
for unwinding-equivalence, and
\textsf{BH}^{\leq}
for the subtyping relation between recursive types in
\mu
-term notation.
Derivations in
\textsf{BH}^{=}
roughly represent bisimulations up to transitivity and symmetry.
Via a connection of
\textsf{BH}^{=}
to a tableaux-like system
\textsf{AK}^{=}
by Ariola and Klop
[
AK96
]
with cyclic deductions
we later recognized that a tableaux system
HS
with loop-detecting deductions of cyclic form similar to
\textsf{AK}^{=}
had already been used earlier by Hüttel and Stirling
in
[
HS91
,
HS98
]

to show that bisimilarity of normed context-free processes is decidable.
In
[
Gra05b
]
we developed a simple coinductively motivated proof system
\textsf{G}^{{=_{\textit{L}}}}
for language equivalence
{=_{\textit{L}}}
of regular expressions.
That system was later refined substantially (using more flexible rules, similar to
\textsf{BH}^{=}
), and generalized (similar to as
\textsf{BH}^{\leq}
generalized
\textsf{BH}^{=}
)
to one for language containment of regular expressions by Henglein and Nielsen in
[
HN11
]
.

However, all of these proof systems use derivations in the form of proof-trees.
Thus they permit cyclic derivations only of ‘palm-tree’ form (ordered trees with backlinks,
[
Tar72
]
).
In contrast,
we will permit cyclic derivations to have the form of general transition graphs.
In this manner we ‘free’ proof-graphs from the requirement to only exhibit ‘vertical sharing’
[
Blo01
]
,
and move close to informal reasoning as used in coalgebra like in
[
JR97
,
Rut98
]
.

The transformations that we construct in Section
6
and Section
7

have been inspired by the proof-theoretic interpretations that we developed in
[
Gra05a
]
between
the proof system
\textsf{AC}^{=}
for unwinding-equality of recursive types by Amadio and Cardelli
[
AC93
]

(a Hilbert-style proof system with a fixed-point rule analogous to
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
)
and
the system
\textsf{BH}^{=}
by Brandt and Henglein.
The transformation from
cMil
to
Mil
in Section
6
is also similar to
one we described in
[
Gra05b
]
that transforms
derivations in
\textsf{G}^{{=_{\textit{L}}}}
into derivations in Salomaa’s system
\textsf{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\textsf{1}}

[
Sal66
]
for
{=_{\textit{L}}}
(where
\textsf{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\textsf{1}}
contains a fixed-point rule just like
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
).

Relation with the conference article.

This article provides significantly more details and explanations than the article
[
Gra21b
]
in the proceedings of CALCO 2021.
Furthermore it contains the following additions of content:

\triangleright

Detailed proofs for the proof transformations from
cMil
to
Mil
(in Section
6
),
and from
Mil
to
cMil
(in Section
7
).

\triangleright

Proof-theoretic explanation of the transformations
as the elimination of rules that are derivable or admissible
(based on Definition
2
, Lemma
1
in Section
2
).

–

For the proof transformation from the coinductive reformulation

cMil
to Milner’s system
Mil

we show that circular coinductive proofs over the purely equational part
Mil
-
of
Mil

are admissible in
Mil
(see Lemma

).

–

For the proof transformation from Milner’s system

to its coinductive reformulation
cMil

we show that the fixed-point rule
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
of

is derivable in

(see Lemma

).

to

(see Corollary

).

).

.

).

An illustration of the difference between
LEE
-witnesses (witnesses of the loop existence and elimination condition
LEE
), and witnesses of ‘layered

’ (LLEE-witnesses)
by an example that is based on different runs of the loop elimination procedure (see Figure
5
).

\triangleright

A section in which we informally link the concept of ‘provable solution’ (Definition
2
)
that is the basis for our concept of ‘coinductive proof’
to a coalgebraic formulation of this concept by Schmid, Rot, and Silva in
[
SRS21
]

(see Section
3
).

Relation with the completeness proof of Milner’s system in
[
Gra22a
]
.

The completeness proof of Milner’s system
Mil
summarized in
[
Gra22a
]
with report
[
Gra22b
]

was finished and written only after the article
[
Gra21b
]
for CALCO 2021.
Indeed, we found the results in Section 6 of
[
Gra21b
]
and here in Section
7

(that instances of the fixed-point rule
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
can be mimicked by LLEE-witnessed coinductive proofs)
in an effort to prepare for that completeness proof. In particular, we wanted to be able to argue for the expedience of the use of LLEE -
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts (see Definition
4
)
despite of the fact that reasoning with LLEE -
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts towards a completeness proof of
Mil
encounters a crucial obstacle
1
1

1

Namely the fact that LLEE -
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts are not closed under ‘
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation collapse’,
an observation that is central for the crystallization procedure sketched in
[
Gra22a
]
.
.
Without any argumentation that links derivations in Milner’s system closely to LLEE -
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts,
it could be conceivable that this obstacle does not have any wider significance.
Namely, it could be entirely specific to the use of LLEE -
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts,
while a completeness proof might possibly be based on quite different concepts.
The situation changed, however, after we realized
that instances of the fixed-point rule can always be modeled (see Lemma
37
)
by cyclic proofs of the shape of guarded LLEE -
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts (see Definition
5
),
and proofs in Milner’s system can be transformed (see Theorem
40
) into meta-prooftrees of such cyclic proofs
(derivations in the system
CLC
, see Definition
5
).
On the basis of these results we could argue that in principle every completeness proof of Milner’s system
Mil
can be routed through (see Section
8
)
arguments in which LLEE -
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts appear front and central.

The completeness proof of
Mil
in
[
Gra22a
,
Gra22b
]

uses additional observations and concepts (above all, a ‘crystallization procedure’ of LLEE -
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts for minimization under
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimilarity),
and is not formulated in terms of the cyclic proof systems that we introduce here.
However, the results of Section
6
,
the transformation of LLEE-witnessed coinductive proofs into derivations in
Mil
(see Proposition
29
)
are of central importance for formulating the completeness proof in
[
Gra22a
,
Gra22b
]
.
Indeed, they prove the lemmas
(E)
(
e
xtraction of provable solutions from guarded LLEE -
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts)
and
(SE)
(provable
s
olution
e
quality in guarded LLEE -
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts)
of the completeness proof as listed in Section 5 of
[
Gra22a
,
Gra22b
]
.

Conversely, we here use another one of the lemmas in Section 5 of
[
Gra22a
,
Gra22b
]
,
the lemma
(T)
(transfer of provable solutions conversely along functional
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulations),
for illustrating the results of Section
6
:
we prove two (specific) completeness properties of LLEE-witnessed coinductive proofs in relation to Milner’s system
(see Corollary
33
).

The completeness proof for Milner’s system
Mil
with respect to process semantics equality of star expressions
implies that the coinductive versions
cMil
and
CLC
of
Mil
that we introduce here are complete (in the same sense) as well.
This is because our main result (see Theorem
40
)
states that
cMil
and
CLC
have the same derivational power as
Mil
.

Overview.

We start in Section
2

with introducing basic definitions concerning the process semantics of regular expressions, and concepts that we will need.
We define star expressions, finite process graphs with
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-transitions,
(
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-)bisimulations and
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimilarity,
and the process semantics of star expressions. Then we introduce equational-logic based, and equation-based proof systems,
with Milner’s system
Mil
and two variants as first examples.
Also, we define when inference rules are derivable or admissible in such a proof system,
and establish easy interconnections.
Finally we define the concept of solution for
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts with respect to an equational proof system.
In Section
3

we link to an insightful coalgebraic characterization of provable solutions of
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts that is due to Schmid, Rot, and Silva in
[
SRS21
]
.
We reformulate it in our terminology, but do not prove it in detail, as our development
does not depend on it.
In Section
4

we explain concepts and definitions concerning the (layered) loop existence and elimination property (L)LEE
from
[
GF20b
,
GF20a
]
,
and recall the ‘
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-chart interpretation’ of star expressions from
[
Gra20
,
Gra21c
]
,
which guarantees
LLEE
.

In Section
5

we introduce ‘coinductive proofs’ over equational proof systems.
We formulate proof systems
CC
and
CLC
with appropriate rule schemes
that permit to use and combine coinductive proofs, and respectively, LLEE-witnessed coinductive proofs.
Then we introduce the coinductive reformulation
cMil
of
Mil
as an extension of the equational part
Mil
-
of
Mil
.
We also establish basic proof-theoretic connections between these new systems.

In Section
6

we show that coinductive proofs over proof systems with derivational power not greater than Milner’s system
Mil
can be transformed into derivations in
Mil
.
We use this fact to obtain a proof transformation from
cMil
to
Mil
.

In Section
7

we demonstrate that every instance
\iota
of the fixed-point rule
\textrm{RSP}^{*}\hskip-1.0pt
of
Mil
can be mimicked by a coinductive proof of the conclusion of
\iota

where (correctness conditions of) that proof may use the equational part
Mil
-
of
Mil
plus the premise equation of
\iota
.
We apply this central observation for defining a proof transformation from
Mil
to
cMil
.
With this transformation and the one constructed in Section
6

we prove that the proof systems
CLC
and
cMil
are theorem-equivalent with Milner’s system
Mil
.

In the final section, Section
8
,
we recapitulate our motivation for introducing coinductive circular proofs,
and summarize our results.
We argue that the coinductive proof systems
CLC
and
cMil
can be viewed as being located roughly half-way
in between
derivations in
Mil
and
bisimulations between process interpretations of star expressions.
We conclude with initial ideas about a proof strategy for a completeness proof of
CLC
and
cMil
,
which would yield a completeness of
Mil
.

2.
Process semantics for star expressions, and Milner’s proof system

Here we fix terminology concerning star expressions,
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts,
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulations;
we exhibit Milner’s system (and a few variants), and recall the chart interpretation of star expressions.

{defi}

[star expressions]
Let
\mathit{A}
be a set of
actions
.
The set
{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
of
star expressions over actions in
\mathit{A}

are strings that are defined by the following grammar:

e,e_{1},e_{2}\>\>{::=}\>\>0\>\mid\>1\>\mid\>a\>\mid\>({e_{1}}+{e_{2}})\>\mid\>({e_{1}}\mathrel{{\cdot}}{e_{2}})\>\mid\>{e^{*}}\qquad\quad\text{(where $a\in\mathit{A}$)}

We will drop outermost brackets, and those that are expendable according to the precedence of star
∗
over composition
{\cdot}
and choice
+
,
and of composition
{\cdot}
over choice
+
.
We use
e,f,g,h
, possibly indexed and/or decorated, as identifiers (for reasoning on the meta-level
like with ‘syntactical variables’
[
Sho67
]
)
for star expressions.
We write
{\equiv}
for syntactic equality between star expressions denoted by such identifiers,
and values of star expression functions, in a given context,
but we permit
{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}
in formal equations between star expressions.
We denote by
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
the set of formal equations
e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}f

between two star expressions
e,f\in{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
.

We define sum expressions
\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}
inductively
as
0
if
n=0
,
as
e_{1}
if
n=1
,
and as
{(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}e_{i})}+{e_{n}}
if
n>0
,
for
n\in{\rm Nature}=\left\{{0,1,2,\ldots}\right\}
.
The
(syntactic) star height

|{e}|_{\scalebox{0.8}{$*$}}
of a star expression
e\in{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)

is the maximal nesting depth of stars in
e
, defined inductively
by:
|{0}|_{\scalebox{0.8}{$*$}}\mathrel{{:=}}|{1}|_{\scalebox{0.8}{$*$}}\mathrel{{:=}}|{a}|_{\scalebox{0.8}{$*$}}\mathrel{{:=}}0
,

|{{e_{1}}+{e_{2}}}|_{\scalebox{0.8}{$*$}}\mathrel{{:=}}|{{e_{1}}\mathrel{{\cdot}}{e_{2}}}|_{\scalebox{0.8}{$*$}}\mathrel{{:=}}\max\left\{{|{e_{1}}|_{\scalebox{0.8}{$*$}},|{e_{2}}|_{\scalebox{0.8}{$*$}}}\right\}
,
and
|{{e^{*}}}|_{\scalebox{0.8}{$*$}}\mathrel{{:=}}1+|{e}|_{\scalebox{0.8}{$*$}}
.

{defi}

[
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts, and charts]
A
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-chart

is a 6-tuple
\langle V,\mathit{A},{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1},v_{\hskip-0.5pt\text{s}},{{\to}},{\downarrow}\rangle

where
V
is a
finite
set of
vertices
,

\mathit{A}
is a set of
(proper)

\emph{actionlabels}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}\notin\mathit{A}
is the specified
empty step label
,

v_{\hskip-0.5pt\text{s}}\in V
is the
start vertex
(hence
V\neq\varnothing
),

{{\to}}\subseteq V\times{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}\underline{\mathit{A}}}\times V
is the
labeled transition relation
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}\underline{\mathit{A}}}\mathrel{{:=}}\mathit{A}\cup\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}}\right\}
is the set of action labels including
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
,
and
{\downarrow}\subseteq V
is a set of
vertices with immediate termination
. In such a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-chart,
we call a transition in
{{\to}}\cap(V\times\mathit{A}\times V)
(labeled by a
proper action
in
\mathit{A}
)
a
proper transition
,
and a transition in
{{\to}}\cap(V\times\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}}\right\}\times V)
(labeled by the
empty-step symbol

{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
)
a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-transition
.
Reserving non-underlined action labels like
a,b,\ldots
for proper actions,
we use underlined action label symbols like
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}\underline{a}}
for actions labels in the set
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}\underline{\mathit{A}}}
;
in doing so we highlight also in
firebrick
transition labels that may involve
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
.

We say that a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-chart is
weakly guarded
if it does not contain cycles of
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-transitions.

By a
chart
we mean a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-chart
\underline{\mathcal{C}}

that is
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-transition free
in the sense that all of its transitions are proper.
We will use the symbols
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
and
\mathcal{C}

(also with subscripts) as identifiers
for
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts, and charts, respectively.
We use the notations
{V\hskip-1.0pt}(\hskip-0.5pt{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\hskip-0.5pt)
, and
{V\hskip-1.0pt}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathcal{C}}\hskip-0.5pt)

for quick reference to the set of vertices of a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-chart
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
, and of a chart
\mathcal{C}
.

Below we define the process semantics of star (regular) expressions as (
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-free) charts,
and hence as finite, rooted labeled transition systems,
which will be compared with (
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-)bisimilarity.
The charts that will be obtained in this way correspond to non-deterministic finite-state automata that are defined
by iterating partial derivatives
[
Ant96
]
(1996) of Antimirov (who did not aim at a process semantics).
Indeed, Antimirov’s result that every regular expression only has finitely many iterated partial derivatives (Corollary 3.5 in
[
Ant96
]
)
guarantees finiteness of chart interpretations as defined below.
We will use the notation
{\mathcal{C}}(\hskip-0.5pt{e}\hskip-0.5pt)
with as meaning ‘the chart induced by (the process interpretation of) the star expression
e
’.

{defi}

The
chart interpretation of
a star expression
e\in{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)

is the
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-transition free chart

{\mathcal{C}}(\hskip-0.5pt{e}\hskip-0.5pt)=\langle{V\hskip-1.0pt}(\hskip-0.5pt{e}\hskip-0.5pt),\mathit{A},{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1},e,{{\to}}\cap({V\hskip-1.0pt}(\hskip-0.5pt{e}\hskip-0.5pt){\times}{\mathit{A}}{\times}{{V\hskip-1.0pt}(\hskip-0.5pt{e}\hskip-0.5pt)}),{{\downarrow}}\cap{V\hskip-1.0pt}(\hskip-0.5pt{e}\hskip-0.5pt)\rangle
,
where
{V\hskip-1.0pt}(\hskip-0.5pt{e}\hskip-0.5pt)
consists of all star expressions that are reachable from
e

via the labeled transition relation
{{\to}}\subseteq{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)\times\mathit{A}\times{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)

that is defined, together with the immediate-termination relation
{\downarrow}\subseteq{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
,
via derivability in the transition system specification (TSS)
{\text{${\mathcal{T}}$}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
,
for
a\in\mathit{A}
,
e,e_{1},e_{2},e^{\prime},e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{2}\in{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
:

\begin{aligned} &\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 11.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{\phantom{${1}{{\downarrow}}$}}\hskip 4.0pt}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to18.00003pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${1}{{\downarrow}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces&\hskip 2.15277pt&\ignorespaces\ignorespaces&\hskip 17.22217pt&\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.47221pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 12.92741pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${e_{i}}{{\downarrow}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to46.84041pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${({e_{1}}+{e_{2}})}{{\downarrow}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\ignorespaces&\hskip 17.22217pt&\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.47221pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${e_{1}}{{\downarrow}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${e_{2}}{{\downarrow}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to58.73859pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 8.4491pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${({e_{1}}\mathrel{{\cdot}}{e_{2}})}{{\downarrow}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces&\hskip 17.22217pt&\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.47221pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\phantom{{{e^{*}}}{{\downarrow}}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to30.03131pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${({e^{*}})}{{\downarrow}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} &\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.57222pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\phantom{a\>\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{a}}}\>1\rule{0.0pt}{12.4pt}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to33.60608pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$a\>\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{a}}}\>1$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces&&\>\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.75891pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 9.03853pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$e_{i}\>\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{a}}}\>e^{\prime}_{i}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to57.36832pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${e_{1}}+{e_{2}}\>\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{a}}}\>e^{\prime}_{i}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\ignorespaces&&\>\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.75891pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 11.92018pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$e_{1}\>\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{a}}}\>e^{\prime}_{1}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to65.44533pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${e_{1}}\mathrel{{\cdot}}{e_{2}}\>\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{a}}}\>{e^{\prime}_{1}}\mathrel{{\cdot}}{e_{2}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\ignorespaces&&\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.75891pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${e_{1}}{{\downarrow}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$e_{2}\>\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{a}}}\>e^{\prime}_{2}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to78.20114pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 12.338pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${e_{1}}\mathrel{{\cdot}}{e_{2}}\>\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{a}}}\>e^{\prime}_{2}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces&&\>\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.75891pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 8.31425pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$e\>\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{a}}}\>e^{\prime}\rule{0.0pt}{11.5pt}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to52.06677pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${e^{*}}\>\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{a}}}\>{e^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\cdot}}{{e^{*}}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}

If
e\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{a}}}e^{\prime}
is derivable in
{\text{${\mathcal{T}}$}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
, for
e,e^{\prime}\in{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
,

a\in\mathit{A}
, then we say that
e^{\prime}
is a
derivative
of
e
.
If
{e}{{\downarrow}}
is derivable in
{\text{${\mathcal{T}}$}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
, then we say that
e

permits immediate termination
.

In Section
4
we define a refinement of this interpretation from
[
Gra21c
]
into a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-chart interpretation.
In both versions, (
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-)charts obtained will be compared with respect to
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimilarity that relates the behavior of ‘induced transitions’ of
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts.
By an
induced
{a}
-transition

v\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8047}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{(}}\hskip 0.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a}}\hskip 0.4pt{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{]}}}}}}w
, for a proper action
a\in\mathit{A}
, in a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-chart
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
we mean
a path
v\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}}}}\cdots\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}}}}\cdot\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{a}}}w
in
\underline{\mathcal{C}}

that consists of a finite number of
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-transitions that ends with a proper
{a}
-transition.
By
induced termination

{v}{{\downarrow^{\hskip-1.5pt{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}
, for
v\in V
we mean that there is a path

v\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}}}}\cdots\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}}}}\tilde{v}
with
{\tilde{v}}{{\downarrow}}
in
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
.

{defi}

[(
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-)bisimulation]
Let
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{i}=\langle V_{\hskip-0.25pt{i}},\mathit{A},{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1},v_{\text{s},i},{{\to}_{i}},{{\downarrow}}_{{i}}\rangle

be
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts, for
i\in\left\{{1,2}\right\}
.

By a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation between
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}

we mean a binary relation
B\subseteq V_{\hskip-0.25pt{1}}\times V_{\hskip-0.25pt{2}}

such that
\langle v_{\text{s},1},\hskip 0.5ptv_{\text{s},2}\rangle\in B
holds (that is,
B
relates the start vertices of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
),
and for every
\langle v_{1},\hskip 0.5ptv_{2}\rangle\in B
the following three conditions hold:

(forth):

\forall v^{\prime}_{1}\in V_{\hskip-0.25pt{1}}\forall a\in\mathit{A}\bigl(\,v_{1}\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8047}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{(}}\hskip 0.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a}}\hskip 0.4pt{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{]}}}}}{{}_{1}}}v^{\prime}_{1}\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;\exists v^{\prime}_{2}\in V_{\hskip-0.25pt{2}}\bigl(\,v_{2}\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8047}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{(}}\hskip 0.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a}}\hskip 0.4pt{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{]}}}}}{{}_{2}}}v^{\prime}_{2}\mathrel{\wedge}\langle v^{\prime}_{1},\hskip 0.5ptv^{\prime}_{2}\rangle\in B\,)\,\bigr)\hskip 1.5pt{,}

(back):

\forall v^{\prime}_{2}\in V_{\hskip-0.25pt{2}}\forall a\in\mathit{A}\bigr(\,\exists v^{\prime}_{1}\in V_{\hskip-0.25pt{1}}\bigl(\,v_{1}\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8047}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{(}}\hskip 0.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a}}\hskip 0.4pt{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{]}}}}}{{}_{1}}}v^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\wedge}\langle v^{\prime}_{1},\hskip 0.5ptv^{\prime}_{2}\rangle\in B\,)\;\;\Longleftarrow\;\;v_{2}\mathrel{{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8047}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{(}}\hskip 0.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a}}\hskip 0.4pt{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{]}}}}}{{}_{2}}}v^{\prime}_{2}\,\bigr)\hskip 1.5pt{,}

(termination):

{v_{1}}{{\downarrow_{1}^{\hskip-1.25pt{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;{v_{2}}{{\downarrow_{2}^{\hskip-1.25pt{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}\hskip 1.5pt{.}

We write
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}\hbox{\kern 0.21529pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{\hskip-1.5ptB}}}\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}

if
B
is a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation between
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
.
We denote by
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}

and say that
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
are
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimilar
,
if there is a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation between
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
.

By a
functional

{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation
from
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
to
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}

we mean a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation
B
between
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}

that is defined by a function
\phi\mathrel{:}V_{\hskip-0.25pt{1}}\to V_{\hskip-0.25pt{2}}

as its graph, that is, by
B={\mathit{graph}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\phi}\hskip-0.5pt)=\left\{{\langle v,\hskip 0.5pt{\phi}(\hskip-0.5pt{v}\hskip-0.5pt)\rangle}\mathrel{|}{v\in V}\right\}
;
in this case we write
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\hskip-1.5pt\mathrel{{{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}\hbox{\kern 0.21529pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}_{\phi}}}\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
.
We write
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}\hbox{\kern 0.21529pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}$\rightarrow$}}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}

if there is a functional
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation from
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
to
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
.

We note that for
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-transition-free
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts the bisimulation conditions specialize to their

usual form:
the induced transitions
{\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8047}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{(}}\hskip 0.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}}\hskip 0.4pt{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{]}}}}}
in (forth) and (back) specialize

to proper transitions
{\xrightarrow{\cdot}}
, and induced termination
{\downarrow^{\hskip-1.5pt{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}
in (termination) specializes to immediate termination
{\downarrow}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
be charts (
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-transition-free
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-charts).
We write
\mathcal{C}_{1}\hskip-0.5pt\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}_{\hskip-1.5ptB}}\mathcal{C}_{2}
, and say that
B
is a
bisimulation between

\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}

if
B
is a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation between
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
.
We write
\mathcal{C}_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}\hskip 1.0pt}\mathcal{C}_{2}
,
and say that
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are
bisimilar

if there is a bisimulation between
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
.
We write
\mathcal{C}_{1}\hskip 0.0pt\mathrel{{\hbox{\kern 0.21529pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\rightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}}}\mathcal{C}_{2}

if there is
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation from chart
\mathcal{C}_{1}
to chart
\mathcal{C}_{2}
).

Let
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
be a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-chart, and
B\subseteq{V\hskip-1.0pt}(\hskip-0.5pt{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\hskip-0.5pt)\times{V\hskip-1.0pt}(\hskip-0.5pt{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\hskip-0.5pt)
.
We say that
B
is a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation
on

\underline{\mathcal{C}}

if
B
is a
{\color[rgb]{0.6875,0.0977,0.0977}1}
-bisimulation between
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a chart, and
B\subseteq{V\hskip-1.0pt}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathcal{C}}\hskip-0.5pt)\times{V\hskip-1.0pt}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathcal{C}}\hskip-0.5pt)
.
We say that
B
is a bisimulation
on

\mathcal{C}

if
B
is a bisimulation between
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}
.

We now define ‘process semantics equality’ of two star expressions
as bisimilarity of their chart interpretations.
We do not introduce the process semantics of star expressions as ‘star behaviors’
(bisimilarity equivalence classes of their chart interpretations) as Milner in
[
Mil84
]
,
but only the relation that two star expressions denote the same star behavior.

{defi}

[process semantics equality]
We define
process semantics equality

as the binary relation
{=_{\scriptscriptstyle\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.5ptP}}}\subseteq{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)\times{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)

by stipulating it, for all
e,f\in{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
,
as bisimilarity of the (
1
-free) chart interpretations of
e
and
f\,
:

e\mathrel{{=_{\scriptscriptstyle\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket_{\hskip-0.5ptP}}}}f\;\;\mathrel{:}\>\Longleftrightarrow\;\;{\mathcal{C}}(\hskip-0.5pt{e}\hskip-0.5pt)\mathrel{\hbox{\kern 0.43057pt\vbox{\hbox{\raise 0.43057pt\hbox{\kern-0.43057pt{$\leftrightarrow$}\kern-0.43057pt}}\hrule}\kern 0.43057pt}\hskip 1.0pt}{\mathcal{C}}(\hskip-0.5pt{f}\hskip-0.5pt)\hskip 1.5pt{.}

{defi}

[proof system
\mathcal{E\hskip-1.0ptL}
,
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\cdot}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based/
\mathcal{E\hskip-1.0ptL}
-based proof systems]
Let
\mathit{A}
be a set.

By an
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based proof system
we will mean a Hilbert-style proof system
whose
formulas
are the equations in
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
between star expressions over
\mathit{A}
.
For an
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based proof system
{\mathcal{S}}

and a set
\Gamma\subseteq{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)

we denote by
{{\mathcal{S}}}{+}{\Gamma}

the
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based proof system whose
rules
are those of
{\mathcal{S}}
,
and whose
axioms
are those of
{\mathcal{S}}
plus the equations in
\Gamma
.

The
basic proof system
{\text{$\mathcal{E\hskip-1.0ptL}$}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
of equational logic

for star expressions over
\mathit{A}
is an
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based proof system
that has the following
rules
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\begin{aligned} \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 10.18242pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\phantom{e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}e\rule{0.0pt}{7.5pt}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\vskip-0.97035pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to27.86804pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.97035pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt{Ref\/l}}}\vskip-0.97035pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}e\rule{0.0pt}{7.5pt}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}&\qquad&\begin{aligned} \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 10.5852pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.316pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\mathord{e}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}\mathord{}$}\hbox{$f$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-1.88701pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to30.50003pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.94257pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt{Symm}}}\vskip-1.88701pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\mathord{f}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}\mathord{}$}\hbox{$e$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}&\qquad&\begin{aligned} \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 11.55743pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}f$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$f\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}g$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-0.9148pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to73.29404pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.91478pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt{Trans}}}\vskip-0.91478pt\hbox{\hskip 22.4769pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}g\rule{0.0pt}{7.5pt}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}&\qquad\begin{aligned} \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 11.55743pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 13.41806pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\mathord{e}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}\mathord{}$}\hbox{$f$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-0.9148pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to56.02016pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.91478pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt{Cxt}}}\vskip-0.91478pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\mathord{{C}[{e}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}\mathord{}$}\hbox{${C}[{f}]$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}\end{aligned}

that is, the rules
Refl
(for reflexivity), and the rules
Symm
(for symmetry),
Trans
(for transitivity), and
Cxt
(for filling a context),
where
{C}[{\hskip 0.5pt}]
is a 1-hole star expression context.

By an
{\text{$\mathcal{E\hskip-1.0ptL}$}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based system
we mean an
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based proof system whose rules include the rules of the basic system
{\text{$\mathcal{E\hskip-1.0ptL}$}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
of equational logic
(additionally, it may specify an arbitrary set of axioms).
We will use the letter
{\mathcal{S}}
as identifier
for
\mathcal{E\hskip-1.0ptL}
-based proof systems.

{defi}

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be an
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based proof system. Let
e,f\in{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
.
We say that
e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}f
is
derivable in
{\mathcal{S}}
,
which we denote here by
e_{1}\mathrel{{=_{{\mathcal{S}}}\hskip 1.0pt}}e_{2}
(instead of the more commonly used notation
\vdash_{{\mathcal{S}}}e_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}e_{2}
),
if there is a derivation without assumptions in
{\mathcal{S}}
that has conclusion
e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}f
.
If
e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}f
is derivable in
{\mathcal{S}}
, we also say that
e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}f
is a
theorem
of
{\mathcal{S}}
.

{defi}

[sub-system, theorem equivalence/subsumption of
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\cdot}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based proof systems]
Let
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
be
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based proof systems.

We say that
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
is a
sub-system
of
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
, denoted by
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\mathrel{{\sqsubseteq}}{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
,
if every axiom of
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
is an axiom of
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
, and every rule of
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
is also a rule of
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
.
We say that
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
is theorem-subsumed by
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
, denoted by
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\mathrel{{\precsim}}{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
,
if every theorem of
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
is also a theorem of
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
,
that is, if
e\mathrel{{=_{{\mathcal{S}}_{1}}\hskip 1.0pt}}f
implies
e\mathrel{{=_{{\mathcal{S}}_{2}}\hskip 1.0pt}}f
, for all
e,f\in{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
.
We say that
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
are theorem-equivalent
, denoted by
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\mathrel{\sim}{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
,
if
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
have the same theorems (that is, if
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\mathrel{{\precsim}}{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
, and
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}\mathrel{{\precsim}}{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
).

For the definitions of the concept of ‘derivable’, ‘correct’, and ‘admissible’ rule in Definition
2
below
for an
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based proof system we introduce an informal concept of derivation rule that will suffice for our purpose.
For abstract formulations of rules, and for the concepts of derivability, correctness, and admissibility of rules we refer:

(i)

to
[
Gra05a
]
,
where these concepts have been gathered and formally treated for Hilbert-style proof systems
(as well as for natural-deduction style proof systems),

(ii)

and to
[
Gra04
]
,
where for ‘abstract Hilbert systems’, systematic connections between these concepts of rules have been studied,
also with respect to how rules can be eliminated from derivations.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be an
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based proof system for star expressions over
\mathit{A}
.
Let
n\in{\rm Nature}
. By a(n)
(
n
-premise) rule
R
for
{\mathcal{S}}
we mean an inference scheme
all of whose instances are of the form:

e_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}f_{1}

\;\;\ldots\;\;

e_{n}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}f_{n}

R

e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}f

with star expressions
e,f,e_{1},\ldots,e_{n},f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in{\textit{StExp}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
.
For such a rule
R
for
{\mathcal{S}}
we denote by
{{\mathcal{S}}}{+}{R}
the
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based proof system
that extends
{\mathcal{S}}
by adding
R
as an additional rule.

{defi}

[derivable, correct, and admissible rules]
Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be an
{\textit{Eq}}(\hskip-0.5pt{\mathit{A}}\hskip-0.5pt)
-based proof system. Let
R
be a rule for
{\mathcal{S}}
.

We say that
R
is
derivable in
{\mathcal{S}}

if every instance
\iota
of
R
can be mimicked by a derivation
\mathcal{D}_{\iota}
in
{\mathcal{S}}

by which we mean that the set of assumptions of
\mathcal{D}_{\iota}
is contained in the set of premises of
\iota
,
and the conclusion of
\mathcal{D}_{\iota}
is the conclusion of
\iota
.

We say that
R
is
correct for
{\mathcal{S}}

if instances of
R
can be eliminated from derivations in
{{\mathcal{S}}}{+}{R}

in the following limited sense:
for every derivation
\mathcal{D}
in
{{\mathcal{S}}}{+}{R}
without assumptions that terminates with an instance of
R

but all of whose immediate subderivations are derivations in
{\mathcal{S}}

there is a derivation
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{S}}
without assumptions, and with the same conclusion as
\mathcal{D}
.

We say that
R
is
admissible in
{\mathcal{S}}

if
{{\mathcal{S}}}{+}{R}\mathrel{\sim}{\mathcal{S}}
holds,
that is, the addition of
R
to
{\mathcal{S}}
does not extend the derivable formulas (the theorems) of
{\mathcal{S}}
.

The definition of ‘
R
is admissible in
{\mathcal{S}}
’
is easily understood to be equivalent with the statement that instances of
R
can be eliminated from derivations in
{{\mathcal{S}}}{+}{R}
without assumptions
in the
un
limited sense:
for every derivation
\mathcal{D}
in
{{\mathcal{S}}}{+}{R}
without assumptions
there is a derivation
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{S}}
without assumptions, and with the same conclusion as
\mathcal{D}
.
Therefore rule admissibility implies rule correctness. This justifies the implication “
\Rightarrow
”
in item
(i)
of the lemma below
that gathers basic relationships between the three properties of rules with respect to a proof system as defined above.

Lemma 1
.

Let
R
be a rule for an
\mathcal{E\hskip-1.0ptL}
-based proof system
{\mathcal{S}}
for star expressions over
\mathit{A}
.
Then the following statements link derivability, correctness, and admissibility of
R
in/for
{\mathcal{S}}\,
:

(i)

R
is admissible in
{\mathcal{S}}

if and only if

R
is correct for
{\mathcal{S}}
.

(ii)

If
R
is derivable in
{\mathcal{S}}
, then
R
is also correct for
{\mathcal{S}}
,
and due to
(i)
also admissible in
{\mathcal{S}}
.
However, rule admissibility and correctness does not imply derivability in general.

Proof 2.1
.

Concerning statement
(i)
of the lemma
we have already argued for the direction “
\Rightarrow
” just above.
The direction “
\Leftarrow
” can be established by showing
that, if
R
is correct for
{\mathcal{S}}
,
then every given derivation
\mathcal{D}
in
{{\mathcal{S}}}{+}{R}

can be transformed into a derivation
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{S}}
with the same conclusion
by eliminating instances of
R
in top-down direction,
using derivation replacements as guaranteed by the defining statement of ‘
R
is correct for
{\mathcal{S}}
’.

For showing the main part of statement
(ii)
, we consider an
n
-premise rule
R
for
{\mathcal{S}}

that is a derivable rule of
{\mathcal{S}}
.
In order to show that
R
is correct for
{\mathcal{S}}
, we have to show that every derivation
\mathcal{D}
in
{{\mathcal{S}}}{+}{R}

that terminates with an instance
\iota
of
R
but has immediate subderivations in
{\mathcal{S}}

can be transformed into a derivation
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{S}}
with the same conclusion.
Let
\mathcal{D}
be such a derivation in
{{\mathcal{S}}}{+}{R}
with instance
\iota
of
R
at the bottom,
as illustrated on the right below.
Since
R
is derivable in
{\mathcal{S}}
there is a derivation
\mathcal{D}_{\iota}
in
{\mathcal{S}}
that derives
the conclusion of
\iota
from its
n
premises.
Then
\mathcal{D}
can be transformed according to the following step:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:prf:it:1:lem:derivable:admissible:rules}\mathcal{D}\left\{\quad\begin{gathered}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 19.50186pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 6.53937pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 9.01563pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\mathcal{D}_{1}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$e_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}f_{1}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{$\;\;\ldots\;\;$}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 9.49423pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\mathcal{D}_{n}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$e_{n}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.2539,0.4102,0.8828}=}}f_{n}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\vskip-0.9148pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{{$\iota$}\hskip 3.0pt}\hbox to96.83783pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.91478pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$R$}}\vskip-0.91478pt\hbox{\hskip 6.53937pt\hbox{\hskip 33.8269pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip
